Q: Who said quality without results is pointless, and results without quality is boring?
A: Johan Cruyff
Q: Didier Drogba is credited with helping end a civil war with a televised plea. In which country?
A: Ivory Coast
Q: Which Swiss club, founded in 1879, claims to be the oldest in continental Europe?
A: FC St. Gallen
Q: Which Scottish club won a Scottish Cup tie 36-0 in 1885, against Bon Accord?
A: Arbroath
Q: Until which year was the Ballon d'Or open only to European players?
A: 1994, it opened up in 1995
Q: The Premier League trophy has two lions on its handles. Who is meant to be the third?
A: The captain lifting it
Q: Two German clubs are exempt from the 50+1 rule because of long company backing. Name either.
A: Bayer Leverkusen or Wolfsburg
Q: Four Spanish clubs are still owned by their members rather than investors. Name any two.
A: Real Madrid, Barcelona, Athletic Bilbao and Osasuna
Q: What do Argentines call the creative number 10 who links midfield and attack?
A: The enganche
Q: What is Feyenoord's academy and training complex called?
A: Varkenoord
Q: What is PSV's training and academy base called?
A: De Herdgang
Q: What is Athletic Bilbao's academy and training base called?
A: Lezama
Q: Which Spanish club left the Sarrià stadium in 1997 and moved into the Olympic stadium on Montjuïc?
A: Espanyol
Q: Which Dutch derby is called the Twentse derby?
A: FC Twente against Heracles Almelo
Q: Which Dutch club's fanatical support stands in the Bunnikside?
A: FC Utrecht
Q: Which Belgian club plays in paars-wit, purple and white?
A: Anderlecht
Q: Which Portuguese club knocked AZ out of the 2005 UEFA Cup semi-final?
A: Sporting CP
Q: Only four European countries travelled to the 1930 World Cup. Name any two.
A: Belgium, France, Romania and Yugoslavia
Q: Which Belgian striker's career was ended by a broken leg months after joining Aston Villa in 2000?
A: Luc Nilis
Q: What does the RKC in RKC Waalwijk stand for?
A: Rooms Katholieke Combinatie
Q: What does the NEC in Nijmegen's club name stand for?
A: Nijmegen Eendracht Combinatie
Q: What does the ADO in ADO Den Haag stand for?
A: Alles Door Oefening
Q: What does the VVV in VVV-Venlo stand for?
A: Venlose Voetbal Vereniging
Q: What does the JC in Roda JC stand for?
A: Juliana Combinatie
Q: Which Belgian club reached the Champions League last 16 in 2016, a first for them?
A: KAA Gent
Q: Which Danish club did Rafael van der Vaart finish his career at?
A: Esbjerg
Q: Which Belgian club from Leuven is known as OHL?
A: Oud-Heverlee Leuven
Q: Southampton handed Ali Dia a debut in 1996 after a phone call to Graeme Souness. Who did the caller claim to be?
A: George Weah, saying Dia was his cousin
Q: Middlesbrough were relegated by two points in 1997 after a three point deduction. What had they done?
A: Called off a game at Blackburn at short notice, saying illness had left them short of players
Q: A 1962 World Cup match in which police had to come onto the pitch repeatedly is known by what name?
A: The Battle of Santiago, Chile against Italy
Q: Referee Ken Aston thought up two pieces of equipment while stopped at traffic lights. What were they?
A: Yellow and red cards
Q: A 1969 war between two Central American neighbours is often named after the World Cup qualifiers that came first. Name either country.
A: El Salvador or Honduras
Q: Barbados deliberately scored an own goal against Grenada in 1994 to force extra time. Why did that suit them?
A: A golden goal counted double, and they had to win by two to go through
Q: With the score level in that Barbados against Grenada game, what were Grenada trying to do in the last minutes?
A: Score at either end, because losing by one goal would have put them through
Q: Indonesia's Mursyid Effendi scored a deliberate own goal at the 1998 Tiger Cup. What were both teams trying to do?
A: Lose, to avoid meeting hosts Vietnam in the semi-final
Q: How many goals did Archie Thompson score in Australia's 31-0 win over American Samoa in 2001?
A: 13
Q: Jaiyah Saelua of American Samoa made history in a 2011 World Cup qualifier. How?
A: She was the first transgender player to appear in a World Cup qualifier
Q: On the day of the 2002 World Cup final the two lowest ranked nations staged their own final. Who played, and who won?
A: Bhutan beat Montserrat 4-0 in Thimphu
Q: Which English league had just two member clubs, who played each other every week of the season?
A: The Isles of Scilly League, Woolpack Wanderers against Garrison Gunners
Q: Torquay were seconds from dropping out of the Football League in 1987 when a police dog bit one of their defenders. What did that achieve?
A: The treatment brought enough added time for Torquay to equalise and stay up
Q: A mascot called Wolfie brawled with men dressed as pigs at Bristol City in 1998. Which club did he belong to?
A: Wolverhampton Wanderers
Q: Swansea's mascot Cyril the Swan was fined 1,000 pounds by the FA. What had he done to Millwall's Zampa the Lion?
A: Pulled its head off and drop kicked it into the crowd
Q: The man inside Hartlepool's monkey mascot stood for mayor in 2002 as a stunt. How did it turn out?
A: He won, and Stuart Drummond went on to serve three terms
Q: Arsenal made the man inside Gunnersaurus redundant in 2020. Which former Arsenal player offered to pay his wages?
A: Mesut Özil
Q: How many of Paul the octopus's eight predictions at the 2010 World Cup turned out to be right?
A: All eight
Q: Colombian defender Luis Moreno was fined and widely condemned in 2011 for kicking what?
A: An owl, the other club's live mascot, which had been struck by the ball
Q: A dog ran onto the pitch in the 1962 World Cup quarter-final and urinated on Jimmy Greaves. Who took the dog home afterwards?
A: Garrincha
Q: Santiago Cañizares missed the 2002 World Cup after an accident in a hotel bathroom. What did he drop on his foot?
A: A bottle of aftershave, which shattered and cut a tendon
Q: Chelsea keeper Dave Beasant was ruled out for weeks in 1993 after dropping what on his toe?
A: A jar of salad cream
Q: Manchester United's Alex Stepney dislocated his jaw during a match in 1975. How?
A: By shouting at his own defenders
Q: Norway defender Svein Grøndalen pulled out of an international after an encounter with what while out running?
A: A moose
Q: Steve Morrow scored the winner in the 1993 League Cup final but never got to lift the trophy. Why not?
A: Tony Adams dropped him while celebrating and he broke his arm
Q: Sergio Ramos dropped the Copa del Rey off the open top bus in 2011. What happened to the trophy?
A: The bus ran over it
Q: Manchester United changed out of their grey shirts at half time at Southampton in 1996. What reason was given?
A: The players said they could not pick each other out against the crowd
Q: Arsenal keeper Dan Lewis let the ball slip in for Cardiff in the 1927 FA Cup final. What did he blame?
A: His brand new jersey, which was greasy
Q: Which club had a statue of Michael Jackson put up outside the ground by its chairman in 2011?
A: Fulham, by Mohamed Al Fayed
Q: Two Nigerian third division games in 2013 finished 79-0 and 67-0 on the same afternoon. Why?
A: Both sides needed goal difference to go up, and all four clubs were banned
Q: Leroy Rosenior was made manager of Torquay in 2007 and sacked ten minutes later. Why?
A: The club was sold right after his unveiling and the new owner wanted someone else
Q: After the 2019 spying row, Marcelo Bielsa called journalists in to Leeds and showed them what?
A: A long presentation of the analysis he did on every opponent
Q: Which English club was taken over in 2007 by a website whose members voted on the team selection?
A: Ebbsfleet United, run by MyFootballClub
Q: Which manager planted a Galatasaray flag in the centre circle at Fenerbahçe in 1996?
A: Graeme Souness
Q: Which manager gave his half time team talk out on the pitch in front of the away fans on Boxing Day 2008?
A: Phil Brown of Hull City
Q: What did Claudio Ranieri promise his Leicester players in return for a clean sheet?
A: Pizza
Q: Stefan Kießling headed a ball wide in 2013 and it went in through a hole in the side netting. What did the German FA decide?
A: That the goal counted and Leverkusen kept the win
Q: An Essex referee was suspended in 2000 for something no official should do. What?
A: He scored a goal himself for the losing side and gave it, then resigned from refereeing
Q: Bury's player-manager Chris Brass managed to break his own nose in 2006. How?
A: His attempted clearance rebounded off his face and into his own net
Q: A 2009 Premier League goal stood after the shot went in off a beach ball. Which two clubs were playing?
A: Sunderland beat Liverpool 1-0, Darren Bent scoring
Q: Johan Cruyff stepped up for an Ajax penalty in 1982 and did not shoot at goal. What did he do?
A: Rolled it sideways to Jesper Olsen, who passed back for Cruyff to tap in
Q: Johan Cruyff wore two stripes on his sleeves at the 1974 World Cup while the rest of the Dutch squad wore three. Why?
A: He had his own deal with Puma and would not advertise Adidas
Q: Johan Cruyff refused to play at the 1978 World Cup. What did he give years later as the reason?
A: An armed kidnap attempt at his home in Barcelona the year before
Q: Johan Cruyff ended up in the number 14 shirt at Ajax when numbers still followed positions. How?
A: A team-mate lost his number 7, so Cruyff gave him the 9 and took 14 from the spares
Q: Dennis Bergkamp would not get on a plane to away matches. What nickname did that earn him?
A: The Non-Flying Dutchman
Q: Which Dutch striker's double family name filled the whole back of his Celtic shirt?
A: Jan Vennegoor of Hesselink
Q: Romania's players nearly all did the same thing to their hair at the 1998 World Cup. What?
A: Bleached it blond
Q: What did Argentina keeper Sergio Goycochea do before penalty shoot-outs at the 1990 World Cup?
A: Urinated on the pitch, for luck
Q: Barry Fry urinated in all four corners of St Andrew's while managing Birmingham. What was the idea?
A: To break a curse said to have been put on the ground when it was built
Q: What did Laurent Blanc do to Fabien Barthez before each France match at the 1998 World Cup?
A: Kissed his bald head
Q: Arsenal restarted the second half of a 2009 Champions League tie against Roma without both centre backs. Why?
A: Kolo Toure always went out last and would not go on until Gallas finished treatment
Q: Which Buenos Aires club did Jorge Bergoglio, later Pope Francis, join as a paying member?
A: San Lorenzo
Q: Which goalkeeper for Coventry and Hereford later announced on television that he was a son of the Godhead?
A: David Icke
Q: Which singer kept goal in Real Madrid's youth ranks until a car crash ended that career?
A: Julio Iglesias
Q: Which French Nobel Prize winner kept goal for Racing Universitaire d'Alger until tuberculosis stopped him?
A: Albert Camus
Q: Which Danish physicist kept goal for Akademisk Boldklub while his brother won Olympic football silver in 1908?
A: Niels Bohr
Q: Which author turned out in goal for Portsmouth AFC under the name A. C. Smith?
A: Arthur Conan Doyle
Q: Petr Čech signed for a club in another sport after retiring from football. Which sport?
A: Ice hockey, as a goalie for Guildford Phoenix
Q: Which 1998 World Cup winner played Stephen Hawking's doctor in The Theory of Everything?
A: Frank Leboeuf
Q: A Euro qualifier in Belgrade was abandoned in 2014 after a drone flew over the pitch. What was hanging from it?
A: A flag showing a Greater Albania
Q: Peru beat Austria 4-2 at the 1936 Olympics and then left the Games altogether. Why?
A: The tie was ordered to be replayed after a pitch invasion and Peru refused
Q: Which dictator's son ran Iraq's football federation and had players beaten for losing?
A: Uday Hussein, son of Saddam
Q: Zaire's players considered refusing to play their last group game at the 1974 World Cup. What was the row about?
A: Money, the bonuses they had been promised were not paid
Q: Haiti's Ernst Jean-Joseph failed the first drugs test at a World Cup, in 1974. What happened to him next?
A: Haitian officials dragged him from the team's quarters, beat him and flew him home
Q: Iran's players handed the United States team a gift before their 1998 World Cup meeting. What was it?
A: White roses
Q: Panama reached their first World Cup in 2017. What did the government do the next day?
A: Declared a national holiday
Q: A Turkish club served a crowd ban in 2011 by letting in only women and children. Which club, and how many came?
A: Fenerbahçe, watched by more than 40,000
Q: Berwick Rangers spent decades in the Scottish league despite one oddity. What?
A: Berwick-upon-Tweed is in England
Q: Which Chelsea chairman had an electric fence installed to keep fans off the pitch in 1985?
A: Ken Bates, though it was never switched on
Q: Chelsea asked their supporters in 2007 to stop throwing one particular vegetable. What was it?
A: Celery
Q: Which club director took the microphone at half time in 2005 and shouted let's be having you?
A: Delia Smith of Norwich City
Q: Sutton United's substitute keeper Wayne Shaw resigned after their 2017 FA Cup tie with Arsenal. What had he done?
A: Eaten a pie on the bench, which a bookmaker had offered odds on
Q: How did John Motson sum up Wimbledon's win over Liverpool in the 1988 FA Cup final?
A: The Crazy Gang have beaten the Culture Club
Q: Iceland's co-manager at Euro 2016 had a second profession. What was it?
A: He was a dentist, Heimir Hallgrímsson
Q: How did Jimmy Bullard mock his own manager after scoring at Manchester City in 2009?
A: He sat his team-mates down on the pitch for a mock team talk
Q: Di Canio caught the ball with an open goal in front of him against Everton in 2000 and won a fair play award. Why did he catch it?
A: Everton's keeper Paul Gerrard was lying injured
Q: What sentence did Eric Cantona first receive for his kung fu kick, before it was changed on appeal?
A: Two weeks in prison, reduced to 120 hours of community service
Q: Robbie Fowler was fined by UEFA in 1997 for a shirt he revealed after scoring. Who was he backing?
A: The sacked Liverpool dockers
Q: John Terry was suspended for the 2012 Champions League final yet appeared in the trophy photographs. How?
A: He changed into full kit and shin pads to lift it
Q: England's players celebrated against Scotland in 1996 with a dentist's chair. What were they re-enacting?
A: A drinking session on their tour of Hong Kong
Q: What did Gary Lineker later admit had happened to him on the pitch against Ireland at the 1990 World Cup?
A: He soiled himself
Q: Manchester United signed Hughie McLenahan from Stockport in 1927 in return for what?
A: Ice cream, donated by a United official to a Stockport fundraising bazaar
Q: Gillingham signed Tony Cascarino from Crockenhill in 1982. What did Crockenhill get?
A: Tracksuits and training kit, instead of a fee
Q: Ronaldo appeared midway through the 2002 World Cup with a shaved head and a triangle of hair. What did he say it was for?
A: To give reporters something to talk about other than his injury
Q: Ronaldo was left off Brazil's team sheet for the 1998 final and then put back in. What had happened?
A: He had a convulsive fit at the team hotel that afternoon
Q: What explanation did Luis Suárez first give for the 2014 World Cup incident with Giorgio Chiellini?
A: That he lost his balance and fell into him
Q: Diana Ross was to score a penalty at the 1994 World Cup opening ceremony, after which the goal would split apart. What happened?
A: She missed, and the goal split apart anyway
Q: FIFA quietly paid the Football Association of Ireland five million euros after which incident?
A: Thierry Henry's handball in the 2009 World Cup play-off
Q: Manchester United skipped the FA Cup in 1999-2000. Where were they instead?
A: Brazil, at the first FIFA Club World Championship
Q: Boca Juniors chose blue and yellow from the flag of a ship in the harbour. Which country's ship?
A: Sweden
Q: What did Everton's Neville Southall do at half time on the opening day of the 1990-91 season?
A: Sat down against a goalpost instead of going in
Q: Sporting Braga's stadium was cut into a working quarry in 2003. What stands behind one of the goals instead of a stand?
A: A sheer granite cliff face, the wall of the quarry
Q: Europe's highest football pitch sits above the village of Gspon in the Swiss Alps. How do the teams get there?
A: By cable car, there is no road up
Q: A much photographed Norwegian pitch in the Lofoten islands has no stands at all. What sits beside it?
A: The sea on almost every side, and wooden racks of drying cod
Q: What is directly underneath the pitch at Monaco's Stade Louis II?
A: A multi storey car park, the stadium is built on top of it
Q: Atletico Madrid's old Vicente Calderon had one stand raised on concrete stilts. What ran underneath it?
A: The M30 motorway, passing straight under the stand
Q: Sapporo Dome staged 2002 World Cup matches indoors on real grass. How did the pitch get in?
A: It was grown outside and slid into the dome on a hovering tray
Q: Vitesse's Gelredome in Arnhem does something with its grass that almost no ground can. What?
A: The whole pitch rolls out of the stadium and sits outside between matches
Q: Schalke's Veltins-Arena was built with around five kilometres of pipework for one product. What does it carry?
A: Beer, piped around the ground to the kiosks
Q: Boca Juniors' Bombonera has three towering stands and one flat vertical wall. Why was the fourth side never built to match?
A: A street ran right up against it, so they had to build straight up
Q: Rayo Vallecano's ground in Madrid is missing something nearly every other ground has. What?
A: A fourth stand, one side of the pitch is just a block of flats
Q: The ground of NK Imotski in Croatia is perched on the rim of what?
A: A karst sinkhole, the Blue Lake, which the ground overlooks from the cliff edge
Q: The rebuilt Bernabeu keeps its pitch somewhere unusual when there is no match. Where?
A: Underground, the grass is lowered in trays into a hangar below the stadium
Q: Tottenham's stadium hides a second playing surface. What is under the grass?
A: An artificial pitch for American football, the grass splits in three and slides away
Q: Hull City opened something at Boothferry Park in 1951 that took supporters right to the turnstiles. What was it?
A: Its own railway station, Boothferry Park Halt, served by match trains from Hull Paragon
Q: Bramall Lane had an open fourth side until 1975. What had been going on there?
A: Cricket, Yorkshire played first class matches on the same ground until 1973
Q: Arsenal's old East Stand at Highbury was listed and could not be knocked down. What was done with it instead?
A: The facade was kept and the stand turned into flats, with a garden where the pitch was
Q: What stands outside Sunderland's Stadium of Light, and what is it there for?
A: A giant miner's lamp, the ground sits on the old Wearmouth colliery
Q: Yeovil Town built a giant killing reputation at Huish thanks to one feature of the pitch. What?
A: A slope of roughly eight feet from one touchline to the other
Q: At which Italian club's ground do away supporters regularly arrive by boat?
A: Venezia, whose Stadio Penzo sits on an island in Venice
Q: The highest altitude football match on record was played in 2017 in the crater of which mountain?
A: Kilimanjaro
Q: Old Trafford was bombed in 1941. Where did Manchester United play their home games for the next eight years?
A: Maine Road, paying rent to Manchester City
Q: Highbury was requisitioned in the war as an air raid post. Whose ground did Arsenal use for home matches?
A: Tottenham's, White Hart Lane
Q: Charlton supporters formed their own political party in 1990. What were they campaigning for?
A: A return to The Valley, and they took thousands of votes in the council elections
Q: Brighton sold the Goldstone Ground in 1997 and then played home matches 70 miles away. In which town?
A: Gillingham, in Kent
Q: Bristol Rovers lost Eastville and spent ten years playing their home games in another city. Which one?
A: Bath, at Twerton Park
Q: Coventry City spent 2013-14 playing home matches 35 miles away after a rent row. Whose ground did they use?
A: Northampton Town's, at Sixfields
Q: AFC Wimbledon was set up in 2002 and needed a squad in weeks. Where were the open trials held?
A: On Wimbledon Common
Q: Darlington's chairman opened a 25,000 seat stadium in 2003 for a club averaging a few thousand. How did it end?
A: It went into administration within months, and George Reynolds was later jailed for tax evasion
Q: Nottingham Forest's main stand burned down during a match in 1968. Whose ground did they move into?
A: Notts County's, Meadow Lane, just across the river
Q: The Faroe Islands' first competitive home match, in 1990, was staged in another country. Where and why?
A: Landskrona in Sweden, because they had no grass pitch of their own
Q: Gibraltar joined UEFA in 2013 and played their early home internationals abroad. In which country?
A: Portugal, at Faro in the Algarve
Q: The 2010 ash cloud grounded flights before a Champions League semi-final. How did Barcelona reach Milan?
A: By coach, driving across Europe
Q: The 1963 big freeze wiped out fixtures for weeks. What did the pools companies invent so coupons could still be settled?
A: The Pools Panel, a room of former players guessing the results of games never played
Q: How many matches did it take to settle the longest tie in FA Cup history, Alvechurch against Oxford City in 1971?
A: Six, around eleven hours of football
Q: Liverpool and Cologne could not be separated in the 1965 European Cup, so a disc was tossed. What happened first time?
A: It landed on its edge in the mud and they had to do it again
Q: How many penalties did Liverpool and Middlesbrough need to settle their 2014 League Cup tie?
A: Thirty, Liverpool winning the shoot-out 14-13
Q: How many goals went in across the ten First Division matches played on Boxing Day 1963?
A: 66
Q: Why was the Wembley pitch a mudbath for the 1970 FA Cup final?
A: The Horse of the Year Show had been staged on it
Q: Wembley Stadium was kept solvent for decades by a sport staged there most weeks. Which one?
A: Greyhound racing
Q: The first football match played under electric light was staged at Bramall Lane in 1878. How was it powered?
A: By portable dynamos brought to the ground, driving arc lamps on wooden towers
Q: The linesman who gave England's third goal in 1966 was always called the Russian. What is named after him in Baku?
A: The national stadium, he was Azerbaijani, Tofiq Bahramov
Q: The referee at Italy against South Korea in 2002 was arrested at a New York airport years later. What for?
A: Smuggling heroin strapped to his body, he was Byron Moreno
Q: Bibiana Steinhaus was the first woman to referee in the Bundesliga. Which other well known referee did she marry?
A: Howard Webb
Q: Belgian referee John Langenus made one demand before agreeing to take the 1930 World Cup final. What was it?
A: A boat ready to take him out of Montevideo within hours of the final whistle
Q: Argentina and Uruguay could not agree whose ball to use in the 1930 World Cup final. How did the referee settle it?
A: One team's ball in the first half and the other's in the second
Q: Jack Taylor held up the kick-off of the 1974 World Cup final. What had nobody done?
A: Put the corner flags out
Q: Anders Frisk retired from refereeing in 2005 after a Champions League tie. What drove him out?
A: Death threats aimed at his family
Q: Paolo Di Canio shoved referee Paul Alcock in 1998. What made the footage famous?
A: Alcock reeled backwards for several steps before finally toppling over
Q: Reading were given a goal at Watford in 2008 that never went near the net. Where had the ball actually gone?
A: Out of play, a couple of yards wide of the post, but the officials gave a goal
Q: The original FA Cup was stolen from a Birmingham shop window in 1895. What did a man confess in 1958 that he had done with it?
A: Melted it down and turned it into counterfeit coins
Q: The World Cup trophy spent the Second World War in a shoebox under a bed in Rome. Who put it there?
A: Ottorino Barassi, an Italian football official keeping it out of German hands
Q: Real Madrid were allowed to keep the original European Cup after 1966. Why?
A: They had won it six times, so a new trophy had to be made
Q: Which English rock band took its name from a South African football club?
A: Kaiser Chiefs, after Lucas Radebe's old club Kaizer Chiefs
Q: Which country's top division has featured clubs called The Strongest, Always Ready, Blooming and Destroyers?
A: Bolivia
Q: Which Peruvian club, named after an Andean people, was a gift to headline writers in Britain?
A: Deportivo Wanka
Q: Which Scottish club's supporters claim their name is the only one in football to appear in the Bible?
A: Queen of the South
Q: Corinthians of Sao Paulo were named in 1910 after a touring side. Where had those tourists come from?
A: London, the amateur club Corinthian
Q: Athletic Bilbao insist on the English word in their name. What did Franco's regime force them to be called?
A: Atletico Bilbao, until they were allowed Athletic back
Q: AC Milan use the English form of the city's name. What did the fascist authorities make them call themselves?
A: Associazione Calcio Milano, the Italian spelling
Q: A group broke away from Milan in 1908 and founded Internazionale. What was the argument about?
A: Milan would not field foreigners, so the new club opened itself to any nationality
Q: Sheffield Wednesday's owl nickname has nothing to do with birds of prey. Where does it come from?
A: Owlerton, the district where Hillsborough was built
Q: Juventus played in pink until 1903. How did they end up in black and white stripes?
A: A member wrote to a friend in England for new shirts and a set of Notts County's arrived
Q: Nottingham Forest chose red in 1865 in honour of a foreign revolutionary. Who?
A: Giuseppe Garibaldi, whose followers wore red shirts
Q: Before settling on blue, Everton played in black with a red sash. What did that get them called?
A: The Black Watch, after the Scottish regiment
Q: What is the tower on the Everton badge?
A: Prince Rupert's Tower, an old village lock up on Everton Brow
Q: West Ham's badge carried a castle for many years. Which building was it?
A: Boleyn Castle, the turreted house that stood beside their old ground
Q: Benfica's crest carries a Latin motto that also appears on American money. What is it?
A: E pluribus unum
Q: AEK Athens carry a double headed eagle on the badge. Which city were the club's founders driven out of?
A: Constantinople, they were refugees in Athens
Q: Norwich's canary nickname is usually traced back to incomers who settled in the city. Which trade?
A: Flemish weavers, who kept the birds
Q: Brighton fans began calling themselves the Seagulls in the 1970s. What were they answering back to?
A: Crystal Palace supporters chanting Eagles
Q: Urawa Red Diamonds take their name and their badge from one company's logo. Which company?
A: Mitsubishi, whose mark is three diamonds
Q: Sheffield United's Greasy Chip Butty song is sung to the tune of which John Denver hit?
A: Annie's Song
Q: Millwall's No One Likes Us, We Don't Care is sung to which Rod Stewart tune?
A: Sailing
Q: St Pauli come out at the Millerntor to Hells Bells. Which band's song do they play when they score?
A: Blur, Song 2
Q: Wales took up a 1983 protest song about the survival of the Welsh language. What is it called?
A: Yma o Hyd, by Dafydd Iwan
Q: Which Bob Marley song did Ajax supporters adopt as an anthem?
A: Three Little Birds
Q: The Seven Nation Army riff became a football chant after the fans of which club picked it up in 2003?
A: Club Brugge, before a European tie against Milan
Q: Norwich fans sing On the Ball, City, often called the oldest football song still in use. What is odd about it?
A: It is older than the club itself, dating from the 1890s, before Norwich City were founded
Q: Why do West Ham supporters sing I'm Forever Blowing Bubbles?
A: A schoolboy player was said to look like the child in the painting Bubbles
Q: The Pompey Chimes, one of the oldest chants in English football, imitates what?
A: The chiming of the Portsmouth town hall clock
Q: Birmingham City took up a Harry Lauder music hall song during a cup run in 1956. Which song?
A: Keep Right On to the End of the Road
Q: Manchester City supporters started a terrace craze in 1988 by bringing thousands of what to matches?
A: Inflatable bananas
Q: Grimsby Town fans joined the inflatable craze of the late 1980s with which object?
A: Inflatable haddocks
Q: Denmark's supporters were given a nickname coined as the opposite of hooligans. What is it?
A: Roligans, from the Danish word for calm
Q: Scotland's fans beat England at Wembley in 1977 and took souvenirs. What did they take?
A: Chunks of the pitch and the goalposts, snapping the crossbar
Q: Europe's oldest organised supporters' group was founded in Split in 1950 and named after something at that year's World Cup. What?
A: The Brazilian torcida, the noisy home crowds in Rio
Q: Union Berlin supporters watched the 2014 World Cup inside their own empty stadium. What did they bring in?
A: Their own sofas and armchairs, carried onto the pitch
Q: Union Berlin supporters have filled the Alte Foersterei every Christmas since 2003 to do what?
A: Sing carols by candlelight, a tradition begun by a handful of supporters
Q: Union Berlin needed their stadium rebuilt in 2008 and had almost no money. How was the work done?
A: Supporters did the building work themselves, for nothing
Q: Manchester United's players arrived in Istanbul in 1993 to a reception at the airport. What were Galatasaray fans holding up?
A: Banners reading Welcome to Hell
Q: Boca Juniors fans say the Bombonera does not tremble. What do they say it does?
A: It beats, la Bombonera no tiembla, late
Q: A girl known as the Toffee Lady walked round the pitch before Everton home games doing what?
A: Throwing free toffees into the crowd
Q: Luton Town banned every away supporter in the 1980s. What did it cost them?
A: Expulsion from the League Cup, because they would not admit visiting fans
Q: Malcolm Allison had a well known model photographed in the Crystal Palace team bath in 1976. How far did Palace get that season?
A: To the FA Cup semi-final, from the Third Division
Q: Zambia won the 2012 Africa Cup of Nations final in Libreville. Why was that city painful for them?
A: Their 1993 squad died in a crash just off the coast there, and the players held a ceremony at the site
Q: Chester's Deva Stadium straddles an international border. Which part of the ground is in Wales?
A: The pitch itself, while the offices and car park are in England
Q: Anfield was not built for Liverpool. Which club played there first, and what happened?
A: Everton, who walked out in a rent row, so their landlord founded Liverpool to fill the ground
Q: The San Siro's official name honours a player. Why is that awkward for the two clubs who share it?
A: Giuseppe Meazza played for both Inter and Milan
Q: English clubs played league and cup football on a Sunday for the first time in 1974. Why were no tickets sold?
A: Charging for Sunday sport was not allowed, so supporters bought a programme and walked in free
Q: In 2009 the Dutch authorities changed the Ajax against Feyenoord fixture for good. What did they take out of it?
A: The away supporters, who were banned from the Klassieker after years of trouble
Q: Bristol Rovers supporters took up a Leadbelly song that had been a hit in 1950. Which one?
A: Goodnight Irene
Q: Leeds United's anthem Marching on Together started life as what?
A: The B-side of the club's 1972 FA Cup final record
Q: Blackpool played the 2010-11 Premier League season at a ground with one side unbuilt. What stood there instead?
A: A temporary scaffolding stand at Bloomfield Road
Q: Leeds keeper Gary Sprake threw the ball into his own net at Anfield in 1967. What did the Kop play over the tannoy at half time?
A: Careless Hands, the Des O'Connor song
Q: Aston Villa's Peter Enckelman conceded the goal that lost the 2002 Birmingham derby. What had he done wrong?
A: Let a team-mate's throw-in roll under his foot, and because he touched it the own goal counted
Q: Massimo Taibi lasted four games as Manchester United's keeper in 1999. What happened in the Southampton match?
A: A tame Matt Le Tissier shot rolled through his legs, and his next away game was the 5-0 at Chelsea
Q: Peter Schmeichel headed a late equaliser for Manchester United against Rotor Volgograd in 1995. How did the night end?
A: United went out of the UEFA Cup anyway, on away goals
Q: Brazil keeper Moacir Barbosa was blamed for the 1950 Maracanazo for the rest of his life. How did he sum that up?
A: He said the maximum sentence in Brazil is 30 years, but that he had served 50
Q: Tim Howard scored from his own area for Everton at Bolton in 2012. What did he then refuse to do?
A: Celebrate, saying he would not enjoy a goal that came at another goalkeeper's expense
Q: Hans-Jorg Butt scored a penalty for Leverkusen against Schalke in 2004 and jogged back. What happened before he got there?
A: Schalke scored straight from the kick-off
Q: Blackburn's Brad Friedel scored a late equaliser against Charlton in 2004. Why was it no use?
A: Charlton scored the winner within a couple of minutes, at the end he had just left
Q: Asmir Begovic scored for Stoke against Southampton in 2013 with a clearance. How far into the match was it?
A: 13 seconds, and the ball travelled about 91 metres
Q: Tottenham keeper Paul Robinson scored against Watford in 2007. How did he manage it?
A: A free kick from inside his own half bounced over the Watford keeper and in
Q: Sunderland keeper Mart Poom scored a stoppage time equaliser at Derby in 2003. Why did the home crowd celebrate too?
A: Poom had spent years at Derby and the fans still adored him
Q: Pat Jennings scored past Alex Stepney in the 1967 Charity Shield. How?
A: A long punt downfield bounced over Stepney and into the net
Q: Gianluca Pagliuca became the first keeper sent off at a World Cup finals, in 1994. Which star did Sacchi take off to get the reserve keeper on?
A: Roberto Baggio, who was caught on camera saying the coach was mad
Q: Sheffield Wednesday were down to ten men 13 seconds into the 2000-01 season. What had happened?
A: Keeper Kevin Pressman handled outside his area at Wolves and was sent off
Q: AZ keeper Esteban Alvarado was sent off in a 2011 cup tie against Ajax. What had he done?
A: Kicked a supporter who ran on and attacked him. The red card was overturned and the tie replayed.
Q: Manchester City's Niall Quinn had a strange afternoon against Derby in 1991. What did he do after his keeper was sent off?
A: Went in goal and saved Dean Saunders' penalty, having already scored at the other end
Q: Harry Kane finished a 2014 Europa League tie against Asteras Tripolis in goal. What had he already done that night?
A: Scored a hat-trick
Q: John Terry finished a game at Reading in goal for Chelsea in 2006. Why?
A: Both Petr Cech and Carlo Cudicini had been injured in the same match
Q: Phil Jagielka went in goal for Sheffield United against Arsenal in 2006. How did it turn out?
A: He kept the clean sheet and United won 1-0
Q: Bobby Moore went in goal in a 1972 League Cup semi-final and saved a penalty. Why is it not remembered as a triumph?
A: The taker scored the rebound, and Stoke won the tie
Q: Manchester United played most of the 1957 FA Cup final without a recognised goalkeeper. Who took the gloves?
A: Jackie Blanchflower, after Ray Wood was hurt in a collision
Q: Aberdeen lost their keeper in the opening minutes of the 2000 Scottish Cup final. Who ended up in goal?
A: Striker Robbie Winters, after Jim Leighton was injured
Q: Argentina keeper Carlos Roa walked away from Mallorca at his peak in 1999. What was he waiting for?
A: The end of the world at the millennium. He came back in 2000 when it did not arrive.
Q: Jens Lehmann held up a 2009 Champions League match against Unirea Urziceni. What was he doing behind the advertising boards?
A: Relieving himself
Q: Gordon Banks lost the sight in one eye in a 1972 car crash. What did he win in the United States afterwards?
A: The NASL goalkeeper of the year award, with the Fort Lauderdale Strikers
Q: Until 1912 a goalkeeper could do something in his own half that would be unthinkable now. What?
A: Handle the ball anywhere in it, not just in the penalty area
Q: Manchester City's Frank Swift did something odd on the final whistle of the 1934 FA Cup final. What?
A: He fainted in his goalmouth and had to be helped up for the presentation
Q: Liverpool keeper David James gave an unusual explanation for a run of blunders in the 1990s. What did he blame?
A: Playing too many computer games
Q: Arsenal's first red shirts were a gift from another club in 1886. Which one?
A: Nottingham Forest, who sent a set to the new club
Q: Liverpool went out in an all red strip for the first time in 1964. Which player did Bill Shankly send out in it to judge the effect?
A: Ron Yeats, because he wanted his enormous centre half to look even bigger
Q: France and Hungary both turned up in white for a 1978 World Cup match in Mar del Plata. What did France end up playing in?
A: Green and white stripes borrowed from a local club, Kimberley
Q: Kevin Keegan and Billy Bremner were sent off in the 1974 Charity Shield. What turned it into a very long ban?
A: They both tore their shirts off as they walked, and each got 11 matches
Q: West Germany had an edge in the rain in the 1954 World Cup final. What had Adi Dassler given them?
A: Boots with screw in studs, changed for the wet pitch at half time
Q: Nottingham Forest's Sam Weller Widdowson introduced shin pads in 1874. What did he make the first pair from?
A: Cut down cricket pads, strapped on outside his socks
Q: The Caribous of Colorado wore what is often called the worst kit ever made, in 1978. What was hanging off it?
A: Leather fringes across the chest, like a cowboy jacket
Q: Gianluigi Buffon had to give up the number 88 shirt at Parma after complaints. What did he say the number meant to him?
A: Four balls, meaning courage, rather than any coded message
Q: AC Milan retired the number 3 shirt for Paolo Maldini but attached one condition. What was it?
A: That one of his sons could wear it if he made the first team
Q: FIFA made Cameroon cover up their kit at the 2002 World Cup. What was the problem?
A: The shirts were sleeveless, so they had to add black sleeves underneath
Q: English clubs had to keep a second set of shirts in the early 1980s. What were they for?
A: Televised games, where shirt sponsors were not allowed to be shown
Q: Alan Ball was paid to wear white boots in 1970. What did he actually run out in when the deal was struck?
A: Adidas boots painted white, because the Hummel pair were not good enough to play in
Q: A famous story has Pele holding up a 1970 World Cup kick-off to tie his laces. What is he said to have been doing?
A: Cashing in a Puma deal, so the cameras would linger on his boots. The tale is much repeated but disputed.
Q: The ball burst during the 1946 FA Cup final. What happened in the 1947 final, which Charlton also played in?
A: The ball burst again
Q: Argentina and Uruguay could not agree on whose ball to use in the 1930 World Cup final. What was the compromise?
A: One ball each half. Argentina led at half time with theirs, Uruguay won with theirs.
Q: Who took the ball home after the 1966 World Cup final, and how did England get it back?
A: West Germany's Helmut Haller. A British newspaper helped buy it back in 1996.
Q: Dynamo Moscow played Arsenal in thick fog at White Hart Lane in 1945. What did the Arsenal players claim afterwards?
A: That Dynamo had twelve men on the pitch while nobody could see
Q: The freeze of 1962-63 wiped out the fixture list for weeks. What was invented so the football pools could keep going?
A: The Pools Panel, a group who decided what the results would have been
Q: A Scottish Cup tie between Inverness Thistle and Falkirk was called off again and again in the winter of 1979. How many times?
A: 29
Q: Halifax Town found a use for their frozen pitch during the big freeze of 1963. What was it?
A: They opened it as an ice rink and charged people in
Q: Two Premier League matches were abandoned in 1997 when the floodlights went out. What was really going on?
A: A betting syndicate was rigging blackouts so the score at the time of the failure would stand
Q: Sheffield United's match against West Bromwich Albion in March 2002 was abandoned. Why could it not go on?
A: United were down to six men, and the 3-0 to West Brom was allowed to stand
Q: Manchester United's final home game of 2015-16 was called off and Old Trafford was cleared. What was the suspect package?
A: A fake bomb left behind after a security training exercise
Q: Goal line technology missed an obvious goal at Aston Villa in June 2020. Why did it fail?
A: Players and the keeper blocked every camera, so no signal was sent. Sheffield United lost the goal.
Q: Manchester City fans invaded the pitch late in the 1974 derby after Denis Law's backheel. What did it achieve?
A: Nothing. The result stood and United were going down whatever happened.
Q: A 1974 FA Cup quarter-final was held up by a pitch invasion with Forest 3-1 up at Newcastle, who then won 4-3. What did the FA order?
A: The tie replayed on neutral ground, twice over, and Newcastle went through anyway
Q: A 1987 European qualifier between the Netherlands and Cyprus had to be played twice. What had stopped the first one counting?
A: A smoke bomb thrown near the Cyprus keeper, so UEFA ordered a replay behind closed doors
Q: Karl Power got himself into a Manchester United line-up photograph at Bayern Munich in 2001. Who was he?
A: A fan and hoaxer who walked out in full kit and stood with the team
Q: A serial pitch invader ran on at the 2010 World Cup final. What was he trying to do?
A: Put a Catalan barretina hat on the trophy. He was Jimmy Jump.
Q: Yeovil Town's old Huish ground gave them a famous edge over visiting league clubs. What was it?
A: A pitch that sloped about eight feet from one touchline to the other
Q: The 1994 World Cup staged matches indoors at the Pontiac Silverdome. How did they get grass onto the floor?
A: It was grown outside in trays and carried in
Q: The Ottmar Hitzfeld stadium in the Swiss Alps sits 2,000 metres up and is reached by cable car. What is strung behind one goal?
A: A net, to stop the ball going down the mountain
Q: Referee Jack Taylor delayed the start of the 1974 World Cup final. What was missing?
A: The corner flags, which nobody had put out
Q: Cameroon's goalkeeping coach was handcuffed on the pitch before an Africa Cup of Nations semi-final in 2002. What did police think he was doing?
A: Placing a charm on the pitch. He was Thomas N'Kono.
Q: Fabien Barthez tried a trick on Paolo Di Canio in an FA Cup tie in 2001. What was it?
A: Standing still with an arm up appealing for offside. Di Canio ignored him and scored.
Q: The North Korea women's team walked off at the 2012 Olympics and held up the game for an hour. What had upset them?
A: The big screen had shown the South Korean flag beside their players' names
Q: Milan's 2005 Champions League quarter-final against Inter was abandoned. What had hit the Milan keeper Dida?
A: A flare thrown from the Inter end. Milan were given the tie 3-0.
Q: A Celtic supporter ran on and brushed past Milan's keeper Dida in 2007. What happened next?
A: Dida went down holding his face, took several minutes of treatment, and UEFA banned him for two matches
Q: Hull City signed Ernie Blenkinsop from Cudworth in 1921 for 100 pounds and one other thing. What?
A: A barrel of beer, about 80 pints
Q: Watford signed the teenage John Barnes from Sudbury Court in 1981. What did Sudbury Court get for him?
A: A set of kit, there was no cash fee
Q: Neymar's lawyers walked into La Liga's offices in 2017 to pay his 222 million euro release clause. What happened?
A: La Liga refused to take the money
Q: What did Luis Figo sign in 2000 that made escaping a move to Real Madrid nearly impossible?
A: A pre-election deal with Florentino Perez carrying a huge penalty if he then refused to join
Q: Winston Bogarde played 12 times in four years at Chelsea. What did he do when the club tried to move him on?
A: Refused to go, and saw out the whole contract collecting his wages
Q: Sunderland put a clause in Stefan Schwarz's 1999 contract banning him from doing what?
A: Travelling into space, his adviser had put money down for a future flight
Q: Sam Hammam wrote a clause into Spencer Prior's Cardiff contract in 2001. What did it require him to eat?
A: Sheep's testicles, a Lebanese dish, with lemon and parsley
Q: Arminia Bielefeld promised Giuseppe Reina a house for every year of his contract. Why did it end badly?
A: The size was never specified, so the club handed him Lego houses
Q: Which Dutch club gave an 18 month old boy a ten year contract in 2011 after watching a video of him?
A: VVV-Venlo, after Baerke van der Meij kicked three balls into his toy box
Q: In 2008 a Premier League club did a deal to scout players using the Football Manager database. Which club?
A: Everton
Q: Benfica hid the teenage Eusebio away in the Algarve in 1960 while Sporting fought for him. Under what name?
A: Ruth Malosso
Q: What did West Brom's Peter Odemwingie do on deadline day in January 2013 without permission?
A: Drove to QPR and sat outside the ground waiting for a transfer that never happened
Q: Why did non-league Stocksbridge Park Steels have to take Jamie Vardy off early in some games?
A: He was wearing an electronic tag and had a curfew to keep
Q: What release clause did Barcelona write into Ansu Fati's contract when he renewed in 2021?
A: One billion euros
Q: Liverpool reportedly wrote an unusual bonus into Mario Balotelli's 2014 contract. What was it for?
A: One million pounds a season for not being sent off repeatedly for violent conduct
Q: The Times ranked Masal Bugduv 30th in its top 50 young players in 2009. What was wrong with him?
A: He did not exist, he was a hoax built from fake reports and Wikipedia edits
Q: Bahrain beat Togo 3-0 in a friendly in September 2010. What did Togo's federation then announce?
A: The team was completely fake, Togo had never sent a side
Q: In 1953 the Spanish federation proposed a compromise over Alfredo Di Stefano that was never carried out. What was it?
A: That he play alternate seasons for Real Madrid and Barcelona
Q: Which Croatia manager has a law degree and played guitar in the rock band that made their Euro 2008 song?
A: Slaven Bilic
Q: Steve Bruce published three novels in 1999. What did their hero, a football manager, do in his spare time?
A: Solve murders
Q: Terry Venables co-invented something called the Thingummywig. What was it?
A: A hat with a wig attached, so women could go out in their curlers
Q: Wolves forward Peter Knowles walked out of football at 23 in 1969 and never came back. Why?
A: He had become a Jehovah's Witness
Q: What was Chris Waddle's day job until Newcastle signed him for 1,000 pounds in 1980?
A: Seasoning sausages and pies in a factory
Q: What job did Rickie Lambert take in 2001 when he was released and had no club?
A: Putting lids on jars in a beetroot factory
Q: Rickie Lambert came on for his England debut at 31 in 2013. What happened almost immediately?
A: He scored the winner against Scotland with his first touch
Q: Which England 1966 World Cup winner spent his working life afterwards as an undertaker?
A: Ray Wilson
Q: Gary and Phil Neville's father had a name that delighted opposing fans. What was it?
A: Neville Neville
Q: On 15 September 1975 Chris Balderstone did two professional sporting jobs in one day. Which two?
A: Batted in a County Championship match, then played a Football League game that evening
Q: Arthur Milton of Arsenal and Gloucestershire was the last man to do what for England?
A: Play for England at both football and cricket
Q: C.B. Fry played in an FA Cup final and Test cricket for England. What world record did he also hold?
A: He equalled the world long jump record
Q: Which two England internationals had a UK top 20 hit in 1987 with the song Diamond Lights?
A: Glenn Hoddle and Chris Waddle
Q: Paul Gascoigne had a UK hit single with Lindisfarne in 1990. What was it called?
A: Fog on the Tyne
Q: Which France World Cup winner went on to race at the Le Mans 24 Hours?
A: Fabien Barthez
Q: In 2016 a former Germany international turned up as a professional wrestler for WWE. Who?
A: Tim Wiese
Q: Shaka Hislop qualified for a very different profession before football. Which?
A: Mechanical engineering, and he interned at NASA
Q: After his career fizzled out at Crystal Palace, which Sweden forward went into business selling vacuum cleaner nozzles?
A: Tomas Brolin
Q: Which France World Cup winner became a European champion in Brazilian jiu-jitsu in 2009?
A: Bixente Lizarazu
Q: Eric Cantona captained France at which sport after retiring from football?
A: Beach soccer
Q: Which former Manchester United and Northern Ireland midfielder was ordained a Catholic priest in 2017?
A: Philip Mulryne
Q: After hanging up his boots, this England striker moved into professional poker. Name him.
A: Teddy Sheringham
Q: Which Southampton and England striker became one of Britain's leading racehorse trainers?
A: Mick Channon
Q: Which Premier League striker was laying bricks for a living while playing for Poole Town?
A: Charlie Austin
Q: What was Neville Southall's job before he made it as a professional?
A: Binman
Q: Which England left back carried on working as an electrician after turning professional?
A: Stuart Pearce, who advertised his services in the matchday programme
Q: Iain Dowie had a degree and a job in a field far from football. Which?
A: Engineering, he had a master's and worked as an engineer at British Aerospace
Q: Which Wimbledon midfielder was working as a hod carrier when the club signed him from Wealdstone in 1986?
A: Vinnie Jones
Q: Ian Wright was working on building sites when Crystal Palace finally signed him. How old was he?
A: 21, remarkably late for a first professional contract
Q: In 1996 Eidur Gudjohnsen made his Iceland debut in a way nobody had before. How?
A: He came on as a substitute for his own father, Arnor
Q: In 2021 two brothers combined for an Iceland goal while their father sat on the bench. Which family?
A: The Gudjohnsens, Eidur's sons Sveinn Aron and Andri Lucas
Q: Manuel Sanchis and his son Manolo share a rare Real Madrid achievement. What?
A: Both won the European Cup with the club, in 1966 and in 1998
Q: Which Ghanaian great saw three of his sons turn professional and all three play for Ghana?
A: Abedi Pele, father of Andre, Jordan and Ibrahim Ayew
Q: Juan Sebastian Veron and his father were known by versions of the same nickname. What was it?
A: The Witch, the father was La Bruja and the son La Brujita
Q: Zinedine Zidane named his eldest son after a Uruguayan he had idolised at Bordeaux. Who?
A: Enzo Francescoli
Q: Which club fielded the three Wallace brothers in the same league side in 1988?
A: Southampton, where Danny, Rod and Ray Wallace played together in October 1988
Q: The Summerbee family managed something very rare in English football. What?
A: Three generations of them played in the Football League
Q: Neil McBain became the oldest player in Football League history in 1947 aged 51. What was his actual job?
A: He was the manager of New Brighton, and went in goal in an injury crisis
Q: Keith Gillespie was sent off for Sheffield United in 2007 seconds after coming on. What had he not done?
A: Touched the ball, play had not even restarted
Q: Walter Boyd came on for Swansea in 1999 and was sent off before the game restarted. For what?
A: A foul before play had restarted, timed officially at zero seconds
Q: Sol Campbell signed a five year contract at Notts County in 2009. How many games did he play?
A: One, and he was gone within weeks
Q: Which Netherlands goalkeeper came out of retirement at 45 in 2016 to play one game for amateurs Noordwijk?
A: Edwin van der Sar, and he saved a penalty
Q: When the maximum wage was abolished in 1961, who became England's first 100 pounds a week footballer?
A: Johnny Haynes of Fulham
Q: George Eastham refused to play for Newcastle in 1960 and took an ordinary job while he fought them. Selling what?
A: Cork
Q: Pele hated the nickname when he got it at school. What was it a mangling of?
A: Bile, a goalkeeper his father's team had
Q: Why was the teenage Ronaldo listed as Ronaldinho in Brazil's 1994 World Cup squad?
A: The squad already had an older Ronaldo, so he became the little one
Q: Which Manchester United defender had already won two All-Ireland football medals with Dublin?
A: Kevin Moran
Q: Tony Cascarino won 88 caps for the Republic of Ireland. What did he later reveal in his autobiography?
A: That he had never been eligible, his mother had been adopted so there was no blood link
Q: Which Equatorial Guinea striker, once capped by Spain at youth level, was top scorer at the 2023 Africa Cup of Nations?
A: Emilio Nsue
Q: Diego Forlan's family had been at the top of Uruguayan football for three generations. Doing what?
A: His father Pablo played at World Cups and his grandfather managed Uruguay
Q: Which Tottenham goalkeeper quit football at 25 saying he was bored, and went into finance?
A: Espen Baardsen
Q: Which Manchester United winger left for Colombia in 1950 and came back known as the Bogota Bandit?
A: Charlie Mitten
Q: Leeds City were expelled from the Football League in 1919 over illegal payments. How were their players got rid of?
A: They were auctioned off in a Leeds hotel
Q: Manchester City's team was broken up in 1906 for illegal payments. Which club signed four of them, including Billy Meredith?
A: Manchester United
Q: Swindon Town won the 1990 play-off final and still did not go up. Why not?
A: They were punished for illegal payments and had the promotion taken away
Q: Tottenham were docked 12 points and thrown out of the FA Cup in 1994. What became of that punishment?
A: Overturned at arbitration, so they played and reached the semi-final
Q: Fiorentina went bust in 2002 and were refounded in the lower leagues. What name were they forced to play under?
A: Florentia Viola, until they bought the old name back
Q: Napoli went bankrupt in 2004. Under what name did Aurelio De Laurentiis's refounded club play at first?
A: Napoli Soccer
Q: Parma were declared bankrupt in 2015 and restarted in Serie D. How long did it take them to get back to Serie A?
A: Three seasons, with three straight promotions
Q: Aldershot went out of business in March 1992 with the season unfinished. What happened to their results?
A: They were expunged from the table as if the games had never happened
Q: George Reynolds built Darlington a 25,000 seat stadium while they sat in the fourth tier. How did that end?
A: The club went into administration and he was later jailed for tax fraud
Q: Portsmouth won the FA Cup in 2008 and were in the fourth tier by 2013. Who bought the club that year?
A: Its supporters' trust, then the biggest fan owned club in England
Q: 1860 Munich lost a play-off in 2017 and then dropped two divisions rather than one. Why?
A: Their investor refused to pay the licence fee, so they were put into the fourth tier
Q: Airdrieonians went bust in 2002. How did the new Airdrie United get a place in the Scottish league?
A: They bought Clydebank and renamed them
Q: MK Dons handed back Wimbledon's trophies and honours in 2007. Who did they give them to?
A: The London Borough of Merton
Q: Long before Milton Keynes, Wimbledon's owner pushed to move the club to another country. Which city?
A: Dublin
Q: Charlton supporters set up a political party in 1990 to force the council's hand over The Valley. What was it called?
A: The Valley Party
Q: Brighton sold the Goldstone Ground in 1997 and then played home games where for two seasons?
A: At Gillingham, about 70 miles away
Q: Robert Maxwell tried in 1983 to merge Oxford United and Reading into one club. What was it to be called?
A: Thames Valley Royals
Q: Assem Allam tried to rename Hull City. What name did he want, and who blocked it?
A: Hull Tigers, blocked by the FA
Q: German rules would not let a drinks company put its name on a club. So what does the RB in RB Leipzig officially stand for?
A: RasenBallsport, meaning lawn ball sports
Q: Red Bull took over Austria Salzburg in 2005 and rewrote the colours and the history. What did the old supporters do?
A: Founded their own SV Austria Salzburg and started again at the bottom
Q: Inter were forced into a merger in 1928 under the Fascist regime. What were the club called afterwards?
A: Ambrosiana, later Ambrosiana-Inter
Q: Fascist Italy banned English words in club names. What was Genoa Cricket and Football Club made to call itself?
A: Genova 1893 Circolo del Calcio
Q: Franco's Spain banned foreign words in club names. What did Athletic Club have to call itself?
A: Atletico Bilbao
Q: Barcelona and Real Madrid both had Di Stefano signed in 1953. What compromise did the Spanish federation propose?
A: That he would alternate seasons between the two clubs
Q: Austria's Matthias Sindelar did not join the unified German team and was found dead in 1939. What was the given cause?
A: Carbon monoxide poisoning from a faulty flue in his flat
Q: England's players gave the Nazi salute before a match in Berlin in 1938. What was the score?
A: England won 6-3
Q: Italy wore black instead of blue against hosts France at the 1938 World Cup. What did they do before kick off?
A: Gave the fascist salute to the Paris crowd
Q: Three rounds of the 1939-40 Football League season were played before war was declared. What happened to them?
A: The season was abandoned and every result expunged
Q: In December 1976 the Athletic Bilbao and Real Sociedad captains walked out carrying a flag that was still banned. Which flag?
A: The ikurrina, the Basque flag
Q: Which dictator's son bought his way into Serie A with Perugia in 2003 and managed one substitute appearance?
A: Muammar Gaddafi's son Al-Saadi
Q: Which Chilean striker refused to shake Pinochet's hand and appeared in the No campaign ads in 1988?
A: Carlos Caszely
Q: Shakhtar Donetsk left the Donbass Arena in 2014 and did not return. Why, and where did they go?
A: The war in the Donbas, so they played in exile, first in Lviv
Q: Which Barcelona great played international football for three different countries?
A: Ladislao Kubala, for Hungary, Czechoslovakia and Spain
Q: Honved were abroad when the 1956 Hungarian uprising was crushed. What did the team do next?
A: Toured on regardless, ending in South America, a tour FIFA refused to recognise
Q: Cuba have several times gone home from the Gold Cup with a smaller squad than they arrived with. Why?
A: Players defect during the tournament
Q: Oleg Blokhin managed Ukraine at the 2006 World Cup while holding what other job?
A: A seat in the Ukrainian parliament, so he had to take the job unpaid
Q: Which Tottenham hero of the 1981 FA Cup final did not play in the 1982 final, and why?
A: Ricky Villa, because Britain and Argentina were at war over the Falklands
Q: Manchester United players who stayed loyal to the players' union in 1909 posed for a famous photo under what name?
A: The Outcasts FC
Q: Scotland's referees went on strike in 2010. How were the weekend's fixtures covered?
A: Referees were flown in from abroad
Q: English league football vanished from television for half of the 1985-86 season. Why?
A: The league and the broadcasters could not agree on money
Q: Brazil banned women from playing football by law. In which year was the ban imposed?
A: 1941, and it stood for close to forty years
Q: FIFA officials were arrested in a Zurich hotel in May 2015. How were they hidden from the cameras on the way out?
A: Hotel staff held up bedsheets around them
Q: FIFA's Chuck Blazer rented a second flat in Trump Tower. What was it for?
A: His cats
Q: Jack Warner tried to prove FIFA was persecuting him by waving an article from which website?
A: The Onion, a satirical site
Q: A comedian gatecrashed a Sepp Blatter press conference in 2015. What did he throw over him?
A: A handful of fake banknotes
Q: Sepp Blatter was re-elected FIFA president in May 2015. How long did he then stay in the job?
A: Four days, before announcing he would go
Q: FIFA's own hired ethics investigator Michael Garcia quit in 2014. Why?
A: FIFA published a summary of his report that he called incomplete and erroneous
Q: Sepp Blatter suggested in 2004 that women's football would draw bigger crowds if the players did what?
A: Wore tighter shorts
Q: Sepp Blatter said in 2011 that racism on the pitch should be settled how?
A: With a handshake at the final whistle
Q: Qatar went back on its promise of beer at 2022 World Cup stadiums. How long before the tournament started?
A: Two days
Q: Nicklas Bendtner was fined heavily and banned by UEFA at Euro 2012. What had he done?
A: Shown a sponsor's name on his underpants after scoring
Q: A South Africa against Senegal World Cup qualifier had to be played again. Why?
A: The referee was banned for life for manipulating the first game
Q: Apartheid South Africa offered FIFA an all white team for one World Cup and an all black team for the next. What came of it?
A: It was turned down and they were suspended
Q: Australia were said to be under a witch doctor's curse from a 1969 qualifier in Mozambique. Who claimed to lift it in 2004?
A: The Australian comedian John Safran
Q: Israel play their football in Europe. Which confederation threw them out, and why?
A: The AFC, in 1974, after member nations refused to play them
Q: Scotland qualified for the 1950 World Cup and did not go. What was their reason?
A: They had said they would travel only as British champions, and England finished above them
Q: Uruguay are the only World Cup holders never to defend the trophy. Why did they stay at home in 1934?
A: In revenge for the European nations who had snubbed their 1930 tournament
Q: Which country is the only World Cup host ever to have had to qualify for its own tournament?
A: Italy, in 1934
Q: Which country had to play a World Cup qualifier in Rome three days before the 1934 finals kicked off?
A: The United States
Q: Which monarch personally picked his country's squad for the 1930 World Cup and got the players time off work?
A: King Carol II of Romania
Q: Austria qualified for the 1938 World Cup and never kicked a ball there. What had happened?
A: The country had been annexed by Germany, so the team no longer existed
Q: Three Sheffield Wednesday players were jailed in 1965 and banned for life for betting on their own side to lose. Name one.
A: Tony Kay, Peter Swan or David Layne
Q: Bruce Grobbelaar won a libel action over match fixing claims. What were his damages cut to at the final appeal?
A: One pound, and he was left with the costs
Q: Two Premier League matches were abandoned in the late 1990s when the floodlights went out. What was behind it?
A: A betting syndicate rigging the lights to freeze the score
Q: A Brazilian referee was exposed as a fixer in 2005. What happened to the eleven league games he had taken charge of?
A: They were annulled and played again
Q: After a violent 1985 cup final the Bulgarian state dissolved CSKA and Levski. Which young forward was banned for life?
A: Hristo Stoichkov, though the ban was later lifted
Q: A 2018 Belgian investigation into fixing and agents' money was given what name?
A: Operation Clean Hands
Q: A Portuguese fixing investigation of 2004 was named after a golden object. Which one?
A: A whistle, the Apito Dourado case
Q: An SPL commission examined Rangers' undisclosed payments in 2013. What did it decide about the titles they had won?
A: They kept them, and the club was fined instead
Q: Joey Barton was banned from football in 2017 for betting. How many bets was he charged over?
A: 1,260
Q: Kolo Toure was banned for six months in 2011 after taking what?
A: His wife's diet pills
Q: Maradona was thrown out of the 1994 World Cup after a failed test. What did he say had been done to him?
A: That they had cut off his legs
Q: Ronaldinho spent five months in custody and house arrest in 2020. In which country, and what for?
A: Paraguay, over a false passport
Q: Real Madrid wiped out enormous debts in 2001 by selling what?
A: Their old training ground, rezoned by the city for office towers
Q: Barcelona sold off chunks of future income in 2022 so they could register players. What did they call these deals?
A: Levers, palancas in Spanish
Q: Sandro Rosell resigned as Barcelona president in 2014 over what?
A: The concealed true cost of signing Neymar
Q: Barcelona were accused in 2020 of paying a company to run social media accounts doing what?
A: Attacking their own players and other critics of the board
Q: Barcelona were revealed in 2023 to have paid millions of euros over many years to which man?
A: Jose Maria Enriquez Negreira, a vice president of the refereeing committee
Q: Massimo Cellino was barred from running Leeds United over unpaid import duty on what?
A: A yacht
Q: Barcelona put UNICEF on the front of their shirt in 2006. What was odd about the deal?
A: Barcelona paid UNICEF, instead of being paid to wear a sponsor
Q: Sunderland put a clause in Stefan Schwarz's 1999 contract banning one particular activity. What?
A: Travelling into space
Q: Adrian Mutu failed a cocaine test at Chelsea and was later ordered to pay the club damages. Roughly how much?
A: About 17 million euros
Q: Bahrain beat Togo 3-0 in a friendly in 2010. What was wrong with the opposition?
A: It was a fake team with no connection to Togo, assembled by a match fixer
Q: A Moldovan teenager called Masal Bugduv made a newspaper list of the best young players in 2009. What was the problem?
A: He did not exist, the whole player was an internet hoax
Q: Which Brazilian club got into the Guinness book for going almost four years without a win?
A: Ibis Sport Club of Recife
Q: A 2002 second tier match was abandoned because Sheffield United were down to six men. Who was their manager?
A: Neil Warnock
Q: Coventry against Bristol City in 1977 kicked off late and finished with both sides passing it about. Why?
A: The Sunderland score went up on the board, and a draw kept them both up
Q: Luton Town began the 2008-09 League Two season on what points total?
A: Minus 30
Q: A Hampshire referee punched a player during a Sunday league game in 1998. What did he do next?
A: Showed himself the red card and walked off, leaving someone else to finish the game
Q: Scottish referee Dougie McDonald resigned in 2010 after admitting what?
A: That he had lied about why he reversed a penalty decision
Q: Antonio Rattin was sent off against England in 1966 by a referee who spoke no Spanish. What reason was given?
A: The look on his face, what the referee called violence of the tongue
Q: Julio Grondona ran Argentine football for 35 years wearing a ring engraved with which two words?
A: Todo pasa, everything passes
